Cos and sin are contiguous of 64 halfs each. They can be loaded as two `float4` loads for cos and two for sin (since 64 halfs = 128 bytes = two float4). Let's just have thread 0 and 1 load them. Or use `tid < 32` to load `__half2` etc. Simpler: have each thread load one element if tid < 64: - if (tid < 64) smem_cos[tid] = cos_ptr[tid]; - if (tid < 64) smem_sin[tid] = sin_ptr[tid]; This uses 64 threads, no bank conflicts (each thread accesses its own bank). Since 64 halfs = 128 bytes, that's tiny. But to maximize speed, we can skip shared memory and just load cos/sin from global. Global memory loads of cos/sin will hit L1/L2 because they are small and reused across heads. Since all threads in the block read the same cos/sin values for the same token, the cache willbroadcast them. Actually, each thread reads its own cos/sin values (8 values). For 256 threads, that's 256*8 = 2048 cos reads and 2048 sin reads per block. But the cos/sin data is only 128 bytes total (64 halfs). So L1 will serve them quickly. Global loads are okay, but redundant loads waste instruction issue bandwidth. Better: load cos and sin into shared memory. Or even better, load them into registers via a warp shuffle broadcast? No, easiest is shared memory. Actually, since cos and sin are token-specific and tiny, we can load them into shared memory and broadcast. Let's do that. But we need to be careful about instruction overhead. Using shared memory adds a few instructions for indexing but it's negligible. Now, vectorized loads/stores for query and key: We can load x and y as `float4` (128 bits). But the values need to be processed as float for multiplication. We can unpack them. Implementation: ```cpp template <typename scalar_t> __global__ void rotary_embedding_kernel( scalar_t* __restrict__ query, scalar_t* __restrict__ key, const scalar_t* __restrict__ cos, const scalar_t* __restrict__ sin, const int num_tokens, const int num_heads, const int num_kv_heads, const int head_size, const int embed_dim) { // embed_dim = head_size / 2 = 64 const int token_idx = blockIdx.x; const int tid = threadIdx.x; const int warp_id = tid >> 5; const int lane = tid & 31; // Load cos/sin into shared memory __shared__ float smem_cos[64]; __shared__ float smem_sin[64]; if (tid < 64) { smem_cos[tid] = __half2float(cos[token_idx * embed_dim + tid]); smem_sin[tid] = __half2float(sin[token_idx * embed_dim + tid]); } __syncthreads(); // Query const int heads_per_warp = 4; const int pairs_per_thread = 8; // must divide embed_dim (64) evenly: 64/8 = 8 threads per head const int threads_per_head = embed_dim / pairs_per_thread; // 8 const int warp_head_base = warp_id * heads_per_warp; const int lane_head = lane / threads_per_head; const int lane_pair = (lane % threads_per_head) * pairs_per_thread; const int q_head_idx = warp_head_base + lane_head; if (q_head_idx < num_heads) { scalar_t* q_ptr = query + token_idx * num_heads * head_size + q_head_idx * head_size + lane_pair; // x_ptr = q_ptr, y_ptr = q_ptr + embed_dim // For pairs_per_thread=8, each thread loads 8 x's and 8 y's. // We can load using float4 if aligned. // scalar_t is half. // 8 halfs = 16 bytes = float4. float4 x4 = *(const float4*)(q_ptr); float4 y4 = *(const float4*)(q_ptr + embed_dim); // Unpack and process __half* x_h = (__half*)&x4; __half* y_h = (__half*)&y4; __half out_x[8]; __half out_y[8]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < 8; ++i) { float x_f = __half2float(x_h[i]); float y_f = __half2float(y_h[i]); float c = smem_cos[lane_pair + i]; float s = smem_sin[lane_pair + i]; float x_rot = x_f * c - y_f * s; float y_rot = y_f * c + x_f * s; out_x[i] = __float2half(x_rot); out_y[i] = __float2half(y_rot); } *(float4*)(q_ptr) = *(float4*)&out_x[0]; *(float4*)(q_ptr + embed_dim) = *(float4*)&out_y[0]; } // Key // Need to process num_kv_heads=8. We can use warp_id again but offset. // Alternatively, we can recalculate key head index. // Let's use a second phase where we map threads 0..63 (2 warps) to key heads. // We can let all threads compute key head and check. // But to avoid divergence within warps, it's better to have contiguous warps. // Since block has 8 warps, we can assign warps 0..1 to key, but we already used them for query. // Actually query uses all 8 warps. For key, we need 2 warps. We can just use warps 0..1 again or any. // It's okay to have conditional `if (warp_id < 2)` but the other warps in the block will be idle. // Divergence is at warp granularity, so no penalty. const int kv_warp_id = warp_id; // 0..1 for key const int kv_head_base = kv_warp_id * heads_per_warp; const int k_head_idx = kv_head_base + lane_head; if (k_head_idx < num_kv_heads) { scalar_t* k_ptr = key + token_idx * num_kv_heads * head_size + k_head_idx * head_size + lane_pair; float4 x4 = *(const float4*)(k_ptr); float4 y4 = *(const float4*)(k_ptr + embed_dim); __half* x_h = (__half*)&x4; __half* y_h = (__half*)&y4; __half out_x[8]; __half out_y[8]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < 8; ++i) { float x_f = __half2float(x_h[i]); float y_f = __half2float(y_h[i]); float c = smem_cos[lane_pair + i]; float s = smem_sin[lane_pair + i]; float x_rot = x_f * c - y_f * s; float y_rot = y_f * c + x_f * s; out_x[i] = __float2half(x_rot); out_y[i] = __float2half(y_rot); } *(float4*)(k_ptr) = *(float4*)&out_x[0]; *(float4*)(k_ptr + embed_dim) = *(float4*)&out_y[0]; } } ``` Wait, using local arrays `out_x[8]` and then taking address with `*(float4*)&out_x[0]` may violate strict aliasing or alignment. `out_x` is an array of `__half` on the stack. `float4` requires 16-byte alignment. Stack alignment for local arrays in CUDA is typically 4 bytes for scalar types, not guaranteed 16. Actually, local variables may be in registers, but taking their address may force them to local memory. PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) { m.def("rotary_embedding", &rotary_embedding_cuda, "Rotary Embedding"); } ''' ``` Wait, `load_inline` in PyTorch expects `cuda_sources` and `cpp_sources`. Now, the kernel code. Let's refine the shared memory and block size. Block size = 256. Let's compute: - num_heads = 32, num_kv_heads = 8, embed_dim = 64. - HEADS_PER_WARP = 4. - PAIRS_PER_THREAD = 8. - threads_per_head = embed_dim / PAIRS_PER_THREAD = 8. - Warp size = 32. 32 threads / 8 = 4 heads per warp. Good. - For query: 32 heads / 4 per warp = 8 warps. 8 warps * 32 = 256 threads. For key, warp 0 processes key heads 0..3, warp 1 processes key heads 4..7. This is fine. The other warps (2..7) skip. Wait, in our query processing, warp 0 processed query heads 0..3. Then in key processing, warp 0 would process key heads 0..3. That's okay, it's just reusing the warps. There's no conflict because query and key are separate buffers. But there is an issue: within a warp, `lane_head` and `lane_pair` are computed the same way for both query and key. For warp 0, lane_head 0..3, lane_pair = (lane%8)*8. For key warp 0: k_head_idx = 0*4 + lane_head = lane_head (0..3). Good. For key warp 1: k_head_idx = 1*4 + lane_head = 4..7. Good. Wait, we wrote `if (warp_id < (num_kv_heads + HEADS_PER_WARP - 1) / HEADS_PER_WARP)`. For num_kv_heads=8, that's 2. So warps 0 and 1 enter, 2..7 skip. The `if` is warp-granular, so no divergence. Inside, `k_head_idx` computed as above. For warp 0, lane_head 0..3 gives heads 0..3. For warp 1, lane_head 0..3 gives heads 4..7. All valid. Now, one more thing: The kernel uses `*(float4*)(q_ptr)`. NVCC may complain about alignment if it can't prove alignment. We can use `__ldg` for loads. Since we are reading from global memory, `__ldg` is good for read-only data (but query is read-write). Actually we read query first and then write back. The read can use `__ldg` if we read through a `const` pointer. But since we will write to the same address, using `__ldg` might not be safe (it's for read-only data). However, the address is the same, but after writing, it's okay. `__ldg` loads from global via texture/constant cache and doesn't need to bypass L1. Writing to `out_x_h[i]` writes into the `float4` register. That's fine. Now, we need to handle the case where `query` and `key` might have different leading dimensions. So they are contiguous in the last two dims if default PyTorch memory layout. `torch.randn(NUM_TOKENS, NUM_HEADS, HEAD_SIZE)` creates a contiguous tensor with stride [NUM_HEADS*HEAD_SIZE, HEAD_SIZE, 1]. Let's write the code string. ```cpp #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #define THREADS_PER_BLOCK 256 #define PAIRS_PER_THREAD 8 #define HEADS_PER_WARP 4 __global__ void rotary_embedding_kernel( __half* __restrict__ query, __half* __restrict__ key, const __half* __restrict__ cos, const __half* __restrict__ sin, int num_tokens, int num_heads, int num_kv_heads, int head_size, int embed_dim, int64_t q_stride_tokens, int64_t k_stride_tokens, int64_t head_stride) { const int token_idx = blockIdx.x; // Query const int q_head_base = warp_id * HEADS_PER_WARP; const int q_head_idx = q_head_base + lane_head; if (q_head_idx < num_heads) { __half* q_ptr = query + token_idx * q_stride_tokens + q_head_idx * head_stride + lane_pair; float4 out_x4; float4 out_y4; __half* out_x_h = reinterpret_cast<__half*>(&out_x4); __half* out_y_h = reinterpret_cast<__half*>(&out_y4); #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < PAIRS_PER_THREAD; ++i) { float x_f = __half2float(x_h[i]); float y_f = __half2float(y_h[i]); float c = smem_cos[lane_pair + i]; float s = smem_sin[lane_pair + i]; out_x_h[i] = __float2half(x_f * c - y_f * s); out_y_h[i] = __float2half(y_f * c + x_f * s); } *reinterpret_cast<float4*>(q_ptr) = out_x4; *reinterpret_cast<float4*>(q_ptr + embed_dim) = out_y4; } // Key const int kv_warps_needed = (num_kv_heads + HEADS_PER_WARP - 1) / HEADS_PER_WARP; if (warp_id < kv_warps_needed) { const int k_head_base = warp_id * HEADS_PER_WARP; __half* out_x_h = reinterpret_cast<__half*>(&out_x4); __half* out_y_h = reinterpret_cast<__half*>(&out_y4); #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < PAIRS_PER_THREAD; ++i) { float x_f = __half2float(x_h[i]); *reinterpret_cast<float4*>(k_ptr + embed_dim) = out_y4; } } } std::vector<torch::Tensor> rotary_embedding_cuda( torch::Tensor query, torch::Tensor key, torch::Tensor cos, torch::Tensor sin) { int num_tokens = query.size(0); int64_t head_stride = query.stride(1); dim3 grid(num_tokens); dim3 block(THREADS_PER_BLOCK); rotary_embedding_kernel<<<grid, block>>>( reinterpret_cast<__half*>(query.data_ptr<at::Half>()), reinterpret_cast<__half*>(key.data_ptr<at::Half>()), reinterpret_cast<const __half*>(cos.data_ptr<at::Half>()), reinterpret_cast<const __half*>(sin.data_ptr<at::Half>()), num_tokens, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_size, embed_dim, q_stride_tokens, k_stride_tokens, head_stride); return {query, key}; } ``` Note: `embed_dim = cos.size(1)` = 64. `head_size` = 128. Wait, we need to be careful about `load_inline`: if `cpp_sources` contains the pybind module, and `cuda_sources` contains the kernel and wrapper, then we shouldn't call `functions` because that will generate another pybind. Actually, if we pass `functions`, PyTorch will auto-generate the pybind code, so we shouldn't provide our own `PYBIND11_MODULE`. Let's verify. From PyTorch docs: `load_inline(name, cpp_sources, cuda_sources=None, functions=None, ...)` If `functions` is specified, it will automatically generate the pybind11 bridge. In that case, `cpp_sources` should only contain declarations or helper code. Or you can omit `functions` and provide the full pybind in `cpp_sources`. It's easier to use `functions` and just define the function in CUDA source. So we can set `cpp_sources=""` (or maybe empty) and `functions=["rotary_embedding_cuda"]`. But the function name in the CUDA source is `rotary_embedding_cuda`. So we do: ```python self._module = load_inline( name="rotary_embedding_h100", cpp_sources="", cuda_sources=CUDA_SOURCE, functions=["rotary_embedding_cuda"], extra_cuda_cflags=[...], verbose=False, ) ``` But `cpp_sources` needs to be a string. If it's empty, it might be okay. Usually people pass an empty string or a comment. Let's check if `load_inline` with empty `cpp_sources` is allowed. It should be. Alternatively, we can put a forward declaration in cpp_sources: ```cpp #include <torch/extension.h> #include <vector> std::vector<torch::Tensor> rotary_embedding_cuda(torch::Tensor, torch::Tensor, torch::Tensor, torch::Tensor); ``` And then keep `functions=["rotary_embedding_cuda"]`. Let's do that. Also, in the CUDA source, we need to include `<torch/extension.h>` as it's a `.cu` file compiled by nvcc. But `load_inline` will wrap the cuda_sources appropriately. One more thing: The correctness test may compare outputs with atol=rtol=1e-2 for fp16. Our kernel does `float x_f = __half2float(...);` and computes in float, then converts back. This matches the original code. If the hidden reference uses `float` math, we should be fine. If it uses half math, the difference could be ~1e-3, still within 1e-2. Now, about `__float2half`: It uses round-to-nearest-even. The original code uses `static_cast<scalar_t>(float_val)` which for `at::Half` also rounds correctly. Should be identical. Potential issue: `float4` assignment in registers: `float4 out_x4; __half* out_x_h = reinterpret_cast<__half*>(&out_x4);`. In device code, taking the address of a register variable may force it to local memory, which is slower. But the compiler might optimize it. Alternatively, we can use `uint4` and bit-cast, or use `__half` array and then load/store as `float4` via `__half2` packing. Wait, can we just write individual elements without vector stores? That would use 8 `STG.16` instructions per thread. Vector store `STG.128` is better. To do a vector store without local memory, we can prepare the `float4` in registers and store it directly. But to set the elements, we need to write to the fields of `float4`. `float4` has fields `x, y, z, w` (4 floats). That's not convenient for 8 halves. Alternative: use `uint4` (4 uints). We can pack 8 `__half`s into `uint4` manually: ```cpp uint4 out_x4; __half* out_x_h = reinterpret_cast<__half*>(&out_x4); ... *reinterpret_cast<uint4*>(q_ptr) = out_x4; ``` Same problem: address of local struct. But actually, in CUDA, `float4` and `uint4` are value types. The compiler can keep them in registers. Taking the address via `reinterpret_cast` inside the same scope might just alias the register set; the compiler may not allocate local memory. It depends on optimization level. At `-O3`, it should be fine. In many kernels, this pattern is used without issues. Another alternative: use `__half2` for each pair and store two `__half2` per half? That would be two 4-byte stores, not as good as one 16-byte store. But we can store four `__half2`s? `__half2` is 4 bytes. Four of them is 16 bytes. We can declare four `__half2` variables and then reinterpret cast to `float4` for storing? That still requires taking address or using unions. Actually, we can write the store as: ```cpp *reinterpret_cast<float4*>(q_ptr) = make_float4( __half2float(out_x_h[0]), ... // no, need half bits in float ); ``` That won't work. Better: Define a struct: ```cpp struct alignas(16) Half8 { __half v[8]; }; ``` Then use it: ```cpp Half8 out_x; ... for i: out_x.v[i] = ... *reinterpret_cast<Half8*>(q_ptr) = out_x; ``` The struct is 16 bytes and aligned to 16. The compiler can keep it in registers. Since it's a local variable with known alignment, the compiler should be able to generate a vector store. Let's use `alignas(16) __half out_x[8];`? `alignas` on local arrays may not work in CUDA. But a struct with `alignas(16)` should. ```cpp struct Half8 { __half v[8]; }; ``` If we declare `Half8 out_x;`, it's an automatic variable, alignment may be 2 unless specified. `alignas(16) Half8 out_x;` might be allowed in C++11 and CUDA. But I'm not 100% sure NVCC supports `alignas` for local variables. It supports `__align__(16)` for types. So we can define: ```cpp struct __align__(16) Half8 { __half v[8]; }; ``` Then use: ```cpp Half8 out_x, out_y; ... *reinterpret_cast<Half8*>(q_ptr) = out_x; ``` This is clean. `__align__(16)` ensures that when stored, the compiler knows it's 16-byte aligned. For local variables, the compiler may allocate it in registers or local memory with 16-byte alignment. Let's use this. Similarly for loading, we can define `const Half8 x8 = *reinterpret_cast<const Half8*>(q_ptr);`. But `Half8` has no constructor. Assignment is fine. Actually, we can just load into `Half8` directly: ```cpp Half8 x8 = *reinterpret_cast<const Half8*>(q_ptr); Half8 y8 = *reinterpret_cast<const Half8*>(q_ptr + embed_dim); ``` Then access `x8.v[i]`. This avoids two separate loads and pointer aliasing. Great. Let's rewrite kernel using `Half8`. ```cpp struct __align__(16) Half8 { __half v[8]; }; // in kernel Half8 x8 = *reinterpret_cast<const Half8*>(q_ptr); Half8 y8 = *reinterpret_cast<const Half8*>(q_ptr + embed_dim); Half8 out_x8, out_y8; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < 8; ++i) { float x_f = __half2float(x8.v[i]); float y_f = __half2float(y8.v[i]); float c = smem_cos[lane_pair + i]; float s = smem_sin[lane_pair + i]; out_x8.v[i] = __float2half(x_f * c - y_f * s); out_y8.v[i] = __float2half(y_f * c + x_f * s); } *reinterpret_cast<Half8*>(q_ptr) = out_x8; *reinterpret_cast<Half8*>(q_ptr + embed_dim) = out_y8; ``` This is very clean. And `Half8` is 16 bytes, so it maps to 128-bit loads/stores. The compiler should generate `LDG.128` and `STG.128`. One more thing: `q_ptr + embed_dim` where `embed_dim` is in elements (halfs). Since `q_ptr` is `__half*`, pointer arithmetic is in elements. `embed_dim = 64`. So `q_ptr + 64` points to the second half. That's correct. Check alignment: `lane_pair` is a multiple of 8. `q_ptr` is `query + token_idx * q_stride_tokens + q_head_idx * head_stride + lane_pair`. Since `q_stride_tokens` = num_heads*head_size = 4096, `head_stride` = head_size = 128, and base alignment is 256 bytes, `q_ptr` is aligned to at least 2 bytes. Is it aligned to 16 bytes? Let's check: - `token_idx * q_stride_tokens` = `token_idx * 4096`. 4096 = 8192 halfs. In bytes, `token_idx * 8192`. 8192 is divisible by 16. So token offset is 16-byte aligned. - `q_head_idx * head_stride` = `q_head_idx * 128`. 128 halfs = 256 bytes. 256 is divisible by 16. So head offset is 16-byte aligned. - `lane_pair` = multiple of 8 halfs = 16 bytes. So 16-byte aligned. Thus `q_ptr` is 16-byte aligned. Great. What about `q_ptr + embed_dim`? `embed_dim` = 64 halfs = 128 bytes. Adding 128 to a 16-byte aligned address yields 16-byte aligned. Good. And for key: same alignment logic. Now, we also need to ensure that `num_heads` is divisible by `HEADS_PER_WARP` (4). 32 is divisible. For key, 8 is divisible. The mapping works. If not divisible, our `if` guard handles it, but the warp layout may have some inactive lanes. Now, compile and test. In `transformers.models.llama.modeling_llama`: ```python class LlamaRotaryEmbedding(nn.Module): def __init__(self, dim, max_position_embeddings=2048, base=10000, ...): inv_freq = 1.0 / (base ** (torch.arange(0, dim, 2).float() / dim)) self.register_buffer("inv_freq", inv_freq) t = torch.arange(max_position_embeddings, device=device, dtype=self.inv_freq.dtype) freqs = torch.einsum("i,j->ij", t, self.inv_freq) emb = torch.cat((freqs, freqs), dim=-1) self.register_buffer("cos_cached", emb.cos()[None, None, :, :], persistent=False) self.register_buffer("sin_cached", emb.sin()[None, None, :, :], persistent=False) ``` YES! `emb = torch.cat((freqs, freqs), dim=-1)`. For key: `num_kv_heads = 8`. `nk = 8 * 64 = 512`. `token_head = token_idx * key_stride + head_idx * head_stride + rot_offset`. `key_stride = key.stride(0) = 1024` (for [512, 8, 128] contiguous). Max `token_head = 511*1024 + 7*128 + 63 = 523264 + 896 + 63 = 524223`. `key[token_head + 64] = 524287`. `key.numel() = 512*8*128 = 524288`. Valid. For cos/sin access: `cos[token_idx * embed_dim + rot_offset]` with `embed_dim=64`. Max index: `511*64 + 63 = 32767`. `cos.numel() = 512*64 = 32768`. Valid. So there was no out of bounds. But the tool might run in a clean environment each time. So each `run_correctness` call might recompile.
